The number of edges of a symmetric edge polytope
Giulia Codenotti, Roberto Riccardi, Lorenzo Venturello

Abstract
. The symmetric edge polytope of a simple graph is a lattice polytope defined as the convex hull of a subset of the type A A roots corresponding to the edges of the graph. In this article we prove a sharp lower bound for the number of edges of the symmetric edge polytope of a graph as a function of elementary graph invariants. Moreover, we characterize graphs attaining this bound. We highlight a connection with the h ∗ h^{*} -polynomial of such polytopes and, motivated by a conjecture of Ohsugi and Tsuchiya, we investigate the behaviour of such polynomial under edge-deletion in the graph.



1.
Introduction

The main object of study of this article is the
symmetric edge polytope
(in short SEP) of a graph. For a fixed finite simple graph
G=([n],E)
, the symmetric edge polytope of
G
is defined as
P_{G}:=\mathrm{conv}\{\pm(e_{i}-e_{j}):\{i,j\}\in E\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
. These polytopes are a rather popular tool arising, sometimes in disguise, in different areas of mathematics such as physics (see for instance
[
CDM18
,
DDM22
]
), the study of discrete metric spaces (
[
Ver15
,
DH20
]
) and optimal transport (
[
ÇJM+21
]
). Our focus lies on combinatorial and geometric properties of these objects, a point of view which has also recently attracted a lot of attention (see
[
HJM19
,
DDM22
,
CDK23
,
KT23
,
MMO25
]
).

Our first result is a lower bound on the number of edges of the symmetric polytope of a graph
G
, denoted by
f_{1}(P_{G})
as a function of elementary graph invariants of
G
.

Theorem A
.

(
Theorem

3.1
)
Let
G=(V,E)
be a connected graph. Then

f_{1}(P_{G})\geq|E|(2|V|-5)-|E_{3}(G)|,

with
E_{3}(G):=\{e\in E\,|\,e\text{ belongs to a 3-cycle of $G$}\}
.

The proof of
A
is graph theoretical, and based of a delicate analysis of the local contribution of each edge of the graph
G
developed in
Section

3
.

Next we show that the lower bound in
A
is sharp and we obtain a rather clean characterization of graphs for which the bound in
A
is attained.

Theorem B
.

(
Corollary

4.3
)
Let
G=(V,E)
be a connected graph. Then

f_{1}(P_{G})=|E|(2|V|-5)-|E_{3}(G)|

if and only if either
G\cong K_{n}
,
G\cong K_{1,1,n-2}
or
G\cong K_{2,n-2}
.

We find a relation between the number
f_{1}(P_{G})
and the
Ehrhart theory
of
P_{G}
. An interesting invariant for any lattice polytope
P
is the function which controls how the number of lattice points grows with the dilations of the polytope. By a seminal result of Ehrhart this function agrees with a polynomial, and is called the Ehrhart polynomial of
P
. It is often more convenient to study the rational function obtained as the associated generating function to the Ehrhart polynomial. Its numerator is the
h^{*}
-polynomial
of
P
, denoted by
h^{*}_{P}(t)
. The
h^{*}
-polynomial of
P
is a polynomial with nonnegative integer s and degree at most the dimension of
P
. In the case of symmetric edge polytopes the
h^{*}
-polynomial has degree exactly the dimension of the polytope, and a very special property: it is
palindromic
, its coefficients satisfy
h^{*}_{i}=h^{*}_{d-i}
, where
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}h^{*}_{i}t^{i}
and
d=\dim P_{G}
.
Our main goal is to investigate a conjecture of Ohsugi and Tsuchiya
[
OT21a
]
predicting certain linear inequalities among the coefficients of the
h^{*}
-polynomial of symmetric edge polytopes. In order to state the conjecture we need to rewrite
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)
in a particular basis for the space of palindromic polynomials of fixed center of symmetry (see
Section

2
for more details). The coefficients of
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)
with respect to such basis can be collected to define a polynomial
\gamma_{P_{G}}(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor}\gamma_{j}t^{j}
called the
\gamma
-polynomial
associated to
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)
.

Conjecture 1.1
.

[
OT21a
, Conjecture 5.11]
The
\gamma
-polynomial associated to
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)
has nonnegative coefficients for every graph
G
.

The
\gamma
-polynomial has been introduced by Gal
[
Gal05
]
to formulate a different but related positivity conjecture. Moreover, nonnegativity of
\gamma
-polynomials has received a lot of attention in combinatorics, as this property sits between the weaker unimodality of the coefficients of the corresponding palindromic polynomial and the stronger real-rootedness of the coefficients (see
[
Ath18
,
Brä15
]
).

Interestingly, the polynomial
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)
, and hence
\gamma_{P_{G}}(t)
only depend on the graphic matroid of
G
(see for instance
[
DJKK24
, Theorem 4.6]
). In
[
DJKK24
]
the authors generalize the definition of symmetric edge polytopes to the broader class of regular matroids, and show that these new objects still have a palindromic
h^{*}
-polynomial. However, it was recently shown that there are regular matroids for which the corresponding
\gamma
-polynomial has a negative coefficient.

1.1
has been verified for either very special families of graphs (
[
OT21b
,
OT21a
]
) or for specific coefficients of
\gamma_{P_{G}}(t)
(
[
DJKKV23
]
). More precisely,
\gamma_{0}=1
and
\gamma_{1}
is equal to twice the cyclomatic number of the graph. In
[
DJKKV23
]
the authors show that also
\gamma_{2}
is nonnegative for every graph, even though there is no elementary interpretation of this number. Their proof strategy relies on a comparison between the quadratic coefficients of
\gamma_{P_{G}}(t)
and of
\gamma_{P_{G\setminus e}}(t)
. In particular, they show that for every graph
G

there exists
an edge
e
such that the quadratic coefficient of
\gamma_{P_{G}}(t)-\gamma_{P_{G\setminus e}}(t)
is nonnegative. This observation, together with a simple inductive argument, yields the nonnegativity of
\gamma_{2}
for every graph (see
Proposition

5.2
)). However, there are graphs and choices of an edge
e
for which the quadratic coefficient of
\gamma_{P_{G}}(t)-\gamma_{P_{G\setminus e}}(t)
is negative (see
Example

6.2
). In order to absorb the potentially negative contribution we propose the following definition.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
G=([n],E)
be a
2
-connected graph. We define

Z_{G}(t):=\sum_{e\in E}(\gamma_{P_{G}}(t)-\gamma_{P_{G\setminus e}}(t)).

In order to prove
1.1
it would be sufficient (in fact stronger) to show the following conjecture.

Conjecture C
.

(Conjecture
5.6
)
Let
G=([n],E)
be a
2
-connected graph. Then the polynomial
Z_{G}(t)
has nonnegative coefficients.

While it is rather immediate to show that the constant and linear coefficient are nonnegative, we make use of
A
to prove the following.

Theorem D
.

(Equation (
17
))
Let
G=([n],E)
be a
2
-connected graph. Then

z_{2}=f_{1}(P_{G})-|E|(2|V|-5)+|E_{3}(G)|,

where
z_{2}
is the quadratic coefficient of
Z_{G}(t)
. In particular, it follows from
A
that
z_{2}\geq 0
.

Perhaps surprisingly, we derive the equality in
D
from a geometric source. First we consider a particular triangulation of
P_{G}
into lattice simplices, and then we employ Betke-McMullen formula to compare
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)
and
h^{*}_{P_{G\setminus e}}(t)
. Besides the result in
D
we propose in
Section

6
a conjectured formula for the polynomial
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)-h^{*}_{P_{G\setminus e}}(t)
which can provide further insights on
1.1
(see
6.1
).

The structure of the article is the following: in
Section

2
we provide the reader with all necessary definitions and background, as well as important results about symmetric edge polytopes.
Section

3
and
Section

4
are devoted to the proofs of
A
and
B
respectively. In
Section

5
we explain the connection with Ehrhart theory and we prove
D
. Finally in Section
Section

6
we formulate new conjectures on the
h^{*}
-polynomial of symmetric edge polytopes.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Lattice polytopes

A
lattice polytope
is the convex hull of finitely many points which are in a lattice contained in
\mathbbmss{R}^{d}
, which is typically
\mathbbmss{Z}^{d}
.
An important property of lattice polytopes is reflexivity, which is first defined in the full-dimensional case, then extended in the general framework. When
P\subseteq\mathbbmss{R}^{d}
is full-dimensional we say that
P
is
reflexive
if

P^{\vee}:=\left\{u\in\mathbbmss{R}^{d}\,|\,\langle u,v\rangle\leq 1\text{ for any }v\in P\right\}

is also a lattice polytope, where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the usual scalar product in
\mathbbmss{R}^{d}
. In general, a lattice polytope
P\subseteq\mathbbmss{R}^{d}
is
reflexive
if there exists a linear isomorphism
\varphi:\text{aff($P$)}\longrightarrow\mathbbmss{R}^{m}
which is also a lattice isomorphism such that
\varphi(P)\subseteq\mathbbmss{R}^{m}
is a full-dimensional lattice polytope and it is reflexive.

For any lattice
d
-polytope
P
we consider
E_{P}(n):=|nP\cap\mathbbmss{Z}^{d}|
. Ehrhart in
[
Ehr62
]
proved that
E_{P}(n)
is a polynomial in
n
of degree
d
, the
Ehrhart polynomial
. Equivalently, the Ehrhart series
Ehr_{P}(t):=1+\sum_{n\geq 1}{E_{P}(n)t^{n}}
is a rational function of the form
\frac{h^{*}_{P}(t)}{(1-t)^{d+1}}
, where
h^{*}_{P}(t)
is a polynomial of degree at most
d
, which is called the
h^{*}
-polynomial
of
P
.

A fundamental theorem by Stanley
[
Sta80
]
states that the
h^{*}
-polynomial has non negative integer coefficients, and Hibi in
[
Hib92
]
proved that a
d
-dimensional lattice polytope
P
is reflexive if and only if its
h^{*}
-polynomial is palindromic, that is,
h^{*}_{P}(t)=t^{d}h^{*}_{P}(\frac{1}{t})
.

2.2.
Triangulations and relations with Ehrhart theory

A
triangulation

\mathcal{T}
of a d-polytope
P
is a simplicial complex whose geometric realization is
P
. The
f-polynomial

f_{\mathcal{T}}(t)=f_{-1}+f_{0}x+\ldots+f_{d}x^{d+1}
encodes the number of faces of
\mathcal{T}
in all dimensions:
f_{i}:=\big|\{\Delta\in\mathcal{T}\,|\,\dim\Delta=i\}\big|
. The
h-polynomial

h_{\mathcal{T}}(t)=h_{0}+h_{1}x+\ldots+h_{d+1}x^{d+1}
is given via the following relation:

f_{\mathcal{T}}(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{d+1}h_{i}t^{i}(1+t)^{d+1-i}.

A d-dimensional lattice simplex is called
unimodular
if its vertices affinely span the integer lattice
\mathbbmss{Z}^{d}
and a triangulation of a lattice polytope into unimodular simplices is called a
unimodular triangulation
. Stanley in
[
Sta80
]
gave an important result which relates lattice polytopes and their unimodular triangulations.

Theorem 2.1
.

[
Sta80
, Corollary 2.5]

If
P
is an integral d-polytope that admits a unimodular triangulation
\mathcal{T}
, then
h^{*}_{P}(t)=h_{\mathcal{T}}(t)
.

We will further make use of the following theorem, known as the Betke-McMullen formula. Indeed, Betke and McMullen in
[
BM85
]
extend the notion of
h^{*}
-polynomial to apply to the support of a simplicial complex, and give a nice formula which extends the previous result of Stanley.

Theorem 2.2
.

[
BM85
, Theorem 1]

Let
\Delta
be a geometric simplicial complex whose vertices are in
\mathbbmss{Z}^{d}
, and let
|\Delta|
denote its support. Then

h^{*}_{|\Delta|}(z)=\sum_{\sigma\in\Delta}h_{\mathrm{link}(\sigma)}(z)\ell^{*}_{\sigma}(z),

where
\ell^{*}_{\sigma}(z)
is the local
h^{*}
-polynomial (in the case of simplices is also called the box polynomial), a related notion to the
h^{*}
-polynomial of a lattice polytope. Defining it for general polytopes has its subtleties (see
[
BKN23
]
), but for simplices there is a nice combinatorial interpretation (
[
Sta92
]
), which will serve as definition here.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
S\subseteq\mathbbmss{R}^{d}
be a
k
-dimensional lattice simplex with vertices
v_{0},\dots,v_{k}
.
The fundamental parallelepiped
\Pi(S)
of
S
is the following
(k+1)-
dimensional polytope,

\Pi(S)=\sum_{i=0}^{k+1}\mathrm{conv}(0,\bar{v_{i}}),

where the sum denotes the Minkowski sum and
\bar{v_{i}}=(v_{i},1)\in\mathbbmss{R}^{d+1}
.

The open fundamental parallelepiped
\overset{\circ}{\Pi(S)}
is the interior of
\Pi(S)
. Then the local
h^{*}
polynomial of
S
is defined as
\ell_{S}^{*}(t)=c_{0}(S)+c_{1}(S)t+\dots+c_{k+1}(s)t^{k+1}
, where

c_{i}(S)=|\overset{\circ}{\Pi}(S)\cap\{\bar{x}\in\mathbbmss{Z}^{d+1}\,|\,\bar{x}_{d+1}=i\}|,

that is,
c_{i}
counts integer points with
(d+1)
-th coordinate equal to
i
inside the open fundamental parallelepiped
\overset{\circ}{\Pi}(S)
.

2.3.
Basics of symmetric edge polytopes

Definition 2.4
.

Given a finite, simple graph
G=([n],E)
, the
symmetric edge polytope
(SEP) is defined to be

P_{G}:=\mathrm{conv}\{\pm(e_{i}-e_{j}):ij\in E\}.

We define
e_{ij}:=e_{i}-e_{j}
. Symmetric edge polytopes live on the hyperplane

H=\{x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{n}=0\},

and their dimension is
n-c
, where
c
is the number of connected components of
G
. We can identify each vertex of
P_{G}
with an edge of
G
with an assigned orientation. In this way every face of
P_{G}
, or a face of a triangulation of
P_{G}
which does not introduce new vertices, can be identified with an oriented subgraph of
G
. We denote by
G(F)
the oriented subgraph corresponding to a face
F
.

In
[
MHN+11
]
, Proposition 3.2, it was proved that any symmetric edge polytope
P_{G}
of a finite simple connected graph
G
is reflexive and
[
HJM19
]
showed also that they also admit regular unimodular triangulations, so its
h^{*}
-polynomial is palindromic and unimodal.

Moreover, since
P_{G}
is reflexive, every affine hyperplane which defines a facet of
P_{G}
is of the form

H_{f}=\Big\{x\in H\,|\,\sum_{v\in[n]}f(v)x_{n}=1\Big\},

where
f:[n]\longrightarrow\mathbbmss{Z}
is called
facet defining
.

In
[
HJM19
]
, Higashitani, Jochemko and Michalek give the characterization of the facets of
P_{G}
in terms of
G
when
G
is connected.

Theorem 2.5
.

[
HJM19
, Theorem 3.1]

Let
G=(V,E)
be a finite simple connected graph. Then
f\colon V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is facet defining if and only if

(i)

for any edge
e=uv
we have
\left\lvert f(u)-f(v)\right\rvert\leq 1
, and

(ii)

the subset of edges
E_{f}=\{e=uv\in E\colon\left\lvert f(u)-f(v)\right\rvert=1\}
forms a spanning subgraph of
G
.

A consequence of the previous theorem is the following.

Corollary 2.6
.

[
HJM19
, Corollary 3.2]

The unimodular simplices contained in a facet
F
of
P_{G}
represented by a function
f
correspond exactly to oriented spanning trees of
G(F)
, that is, oriented spanning trees consisting of oriented edges
\vec{vw}
such that
f(v)-f(w)=1
.

Further, in
[
HJM19
]
, it is shown that symmetric edge polytopes have regular unimodular
triangulations. We will outline their construction here and call these triangulations

HJM triangulations
. The construction is given in terms of its Stanley-Reisner ideal: that
is, a squarefree ideal
J
in the ring
K[x_{\alpha}\,:\,\alpha\in P_{G}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
, and the corresponding triangulation
\Gamma
of
P_{G}
consists of all the subsets
S\subseteq P_{G}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that conv(
S
) is a simplex and
\prod_{\alpha\in S}x_{\alpha}\not\in J
.

In order to construct such
J
, remember at first that the integer points of
P_{G}
are precisely
\{0\}\cup\{\pm e_{ij}\,:\,ij\in E(G)\}
, so
K[x_{\alpha}\,:\,\alpha\in P_{G}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
is naturally identified as

K[\{x_{e},y_{e}\}_{e\in E(G)}\cup\{z\}]
where
z
is the variable associated to the origin.

In order to simplify notation, in the following, for any oriented edge
e
, we denote by
p_{e}
the corresponding variable, i.e.
p_{e}=x_{e}
or
p_{e}=y_{e}
depending on the orientation. We also set
q_{e}
to be equal to the variable with the opposite orientation, i.e.
\{p_{e},q_{e}\}=\{x_{e},y_{e}\}
.

Then, fix a total order
<
on
E(G)
that induces a total order on the variables in
K[\{x_{e},y_{e}\}_{e\in E(G)}\cup\{z\}]
, hence consider the degrevlex order respect to
<
on the monomials.

J
will be the initial ideal respect to
<
of a toric ideal
I_{P_{G}}
which Gröbner basis is given by the following Proposition.

Proposition 2.7
.

[
HJM19
, Proposition 3.8]

Let
z<x_{e_{1}}<y_{e_{1}}<\dots<x_{e_{k}}<y_{e_{k}}
be an order on the edges. Then the following collection of three types of binomials forms a Gröbner basis of the toric ideal of
P_{G}
with respect to the degrevlex order:

(1)

For every
2k
-cycle
C
, with fixed orientation, and any
k
-element subset
I
of edges of
C
not containing the smallest edge

\prod_{e\in I}p_{e}-\prod_{e\in C\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to3.6pt{\vbox to6.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-0.3pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}I}q_{e}.

(2)

For every
(2k+1)
-cycle
C
, with fixed orientation, and any
(k+1)
-element subset
I
of edges of
C

\prod_{e\in I}p_{e}-z\prod_{e\in C\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to3.6pt{\vbox to6.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-0.3pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }

(3)

For any edge
e

x_{e}y_{e}-z^{2}\,.

The leading monomial is always chosen to have positive sign.

It is important to note that every maximal simplex of the HJM triangulation contains
the origin. In fact, given a monomial
m
without the variable
z
, we have

m\in\text{in}_{<}(I_{P_{G}})\Longleftrightarrow z\cdot m\in\text{in}_{<}(I_{P_{G}}).

2.4.
Real-rootedness, unimodality and
\gamma
-positivity

Let
a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{d}
be a sequence of non-negative real numbers.
The polynomial
f(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}a_{i}t^{i}
of degree
d
is
real-rooted
if all its roots are real. If
f
is real-rooted, then the sequence of the coefficients
(a_{i})_{i=0}^{d}
is
log-concave
, which means that
a_{i}^{2}\geq a_{i-1}a_{i+1}
for all
i=1,\ldots,d-1

[
Sta89
]
. A non-negative log-concave sequence is also
unimodal
, that is,
a_{0}\leq a_{1}\leq\cdots\leq a_{k}\geq a_{k+1}\geq\cdots\geq a_{d}
for some
k
.

The polynomial
f
is said to be
palindromic
if
f(t)=t^{d}f(\frac{1}{t})
. A palindromic polynomial can be written as follows, for unique real numbers
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}
:

f(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\gamma_{i}t^{i}(1+t)^{d-2i}.

The polynomial
\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}t^{i}
is called the
\gamma
-polynomial
of
f
. We say that the polynomial
f(t)
is
\gamma
-nonnegative
if
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\geq 0
.
We have that a
\gamma
-nonnegative polynomial is real-rooted if and only if its
\gamma
-polynomial is real-rooted.

It can be seen also that a
\gamma
-nonnegative polynomial is unimodal, so in the palindromic case, even if
\gamma
-positivity and real-rootedness are different notions, they share some nice combinatorial properties.

3.
Lower bound on the number of edges of symmetric edge polytopes

This section is devoted to proving a lower bound on
f_{1}(P_{G})
, the number of edges of the symmetric edge polytope of a connected graph
G=(V,E)
.
We let

E_{3}(G):=\{l\in E\,|\,l\text{ is in a 3-cycle of $G$}\}.

We will prove the following lower bound on
f_{1}(P_{G})
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
G
be a connected graph. Then

f_{1}(P_{G})\geq|E|(2|V|-5)-|E_{3}(G)|.

As a first step, we characterize
f_{1}(P_{G})
purely in terms of the graph. We use the notation
OE(G)
to denote the set of all possible orientations of edges of
G
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For a connected graph
G
, we have

f_{1}(P_{G})=|\underbrace{\bigl\{\{\vec{l_{1}},\vec{l_{2}}\}\,|\,\vec{l_{1}},\vec{l_{2}}\in OE(G),\,l_{1}\neq l_{2}\text{ , $\vec{l_{1}},\vec{l_{2}}$ not in a common oriented 3 or 4-cycle of $G$}\bigr\}}_{=:F_{1}(G)}|.

Proof.

Consider two vertices
e_{ij}
and
e_{kl}
of
P_{G}
, which correspond to two oriented edges
\vec{l_{1}}=(i,j)
and
\vec{l_{2}}=(k,l)
of
G
. These two vertices form an edge of
P_{G}
if there is a hyperplane
H_{c}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid c^{T}\cdot x=b\}
which supports
P_{G}
and contains
e_{ij}
and
e_{kl}
and no other vertex of
P_{G}
. Since
c^{T}\cdot e_{st}=c_{t}-c_{s}
for any vertex
e_{st}
, we need to prove that there exists a vector
c
and scalar
b
which satisfy

(1)

\displaystyle c_{j}-c_{i}=c_{l}-c_{k}=b

(2)

\displaystyle c_{s}-c_{t}<b\text{ for all }e_{st}\text{ other vertices of }P_{G}

exactly when the conditions above are satisfied, that is, when the oriented edges
(i,j)
and
(k,l)
of the graph are not in oriented
3-
or
4-
cycles.

We distinguish two cases: when
i,j,k,l
are
4
distinct vertices of the graph
G
and when two coincide to give us exactly
3
distinct vertices of
G
.

If
i,j,k,l
are distinct, assume without loss of generality that they are
1,2,3,4
; in this case, we have to show that the two oriented edges
(i,j)
and
(k,l)
are not in an oriented
4
-cycle. In order to satisfy the conditions
1
,
c
must be of the form
(-a,b-a,a,b+a,c_{5},\dots,c_{n})
(up to adding a constant to all entries), with
|c_{i}|<a
.
Then to make sure that all vertices of
P_{G}
except the two chosen ones are on the hyperplane, the only candidates are
(l,i)
and
(j,k)
. Their scalar product with
c
is
b-2a
and
b+2a
, so both cannot correspond to vertices of
P_{G}
, as we wanted.

The case where we have three vertices
i,j,k
is similar.
∎

We can then reformulate
Theorem

3.1
by defining the function

(3)

z_{2}(G):=f_{1}(P_{G})-|E|(2|V|-5)-|E_{3}(G)|.

The theorem is then equivalent to showing that
z_{2}(G)\geq 0
.

To prove this, we split
z_{2}(G)
into a sum whose terms depend on single edges of
G
, and upper bound each of these terms. First of all, for any pair of edges
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
of
G
, we define

f(G,l_{1},l_{2}):=\big|\bigl\{\{\vec{l_{1}},\vec{l_{2}}\}\,|\,\vec{l_{i}}\text{ is an orientation of $l_{i}$ for $i=1,2$}\bigr\}\cap F_{1}(G)\big|

Remark 3.3
.

f(G,l_{1},l_{2})\in\{0,2,4\}
for any
l_{1},l_{2}\in E
. In particular:

(i)

f(G,l_{1},l_{2})=0
if
l_{1}=l_{2}
, or if
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
are in a 4-clique of
G
and they are not adjacent;

(ii)

f(G,l_{1},l_{2})=2
if
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
are in a 3-cycle of
G
, or the induced subgraph of
G
respect to the vertices of
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
contains a 4-cycle but it’s not a 4-clique;

(iii)

f(G,l_{1},l_{2})=4
if
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
are not in any 3 or 4-cycle of
G
.

Now, for any
l\in E
, we define the
contribution of
l
in
f_{1}(G)

as the set

F(G,l):=\bigl\{\{\vec{l_{1}},\vec{l_{2}}\}\in f_{1}(G)\,|\,\vec{l_{1}}\text{ is an orientation of }l\bigr\},

and, setting
v_{G}:=|V(G)|
, we define the function
Z(G,l)
as follows

(4)

Z(G,l):=|F(G,l)|-2(2v_{G}-5)-2\cdot\mathbb{1}_{E_{3}(G)}(l)

where
\mathbb{1}_{E_{3}}
is the indicator function of
E_{3}
.

It’s clear by the definitions that

(5)

|F(G,l)|=\sum_{f\in E}f(G,l,f),

and

(6)

z_{2}(G)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l\in E}Z(G,l).

What we will do is to show that
Z(G,l)
has a good behavior, as stated in the following lemma.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
G
be a connected graph. Then, for at most one
l\in E
it holds that

(7)

Z(G,l)<0,

and if there is such an
l
, then there exists
l^{\prime}\in E
such that

(8)

Z(G,l)+Z(G,l^{\prime})\geq 0.

In particular Proposition
3.1
is proven if we apply Proposition
3.4
in
6
.

What we want to do is to ”compute”
Z(G,l)
in a recursive way, starting from the subgraph
G_{0}=\{l\}
, and then construct a sequence of subgraphs of
G

\{l\}=G_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{i_{0}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{i_{1}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{i_{k-1}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{k}=G,

where
G_{i+1}
is obtained by
G_{i}
performing one of the following moves:

m1)
:

Adding a new vertex
w
and a new edge
\{v,w\}
, where
v
is a vertex of
G_{i}
.

m2)
:

Adding an edge of
G
to
G_{i}
with vertices in
V(G_{i})
.

It’s important to observe that
m1
preserves the Z function: in other words, if
G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m1}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{i+1}
, then
Z(G_{i+1},l)=Z(G_{i},l)
. This is true because
v_{G_{i+1}}=v_{G_{i}}+1
and the added edge
\{v,w\}
is not in any cycle of
G_{i+1}
, giving us
f(G_{i+1},l,\{v,w\})=4
. For
m2
the situation is more complicated: there are some cases in which, respectively, the Z function increases, decreases, or remains the same. Here some examples are reported.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
G_{i}\,=
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
=\,G_{i+1}
m_{2}
l
l

Figure 1.
Z
decreases:
Z(G_{i},l)=2
, meanwhile
Z(G_{i+1},l)=0

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
G_{i}\,=
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
=\,G_{i+1}
m_{2}
l
l

Figure 2.
Z(G_{i},l)=0=Z(G_{i+1},l)

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
G_{i}\,=
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
=\,G_{i+1}
m_{2}
l
l

Figure 3.
Z
increases:
Z(G_{i},l)=2
, meanwhile
Z(G_{i+1},l)=6

However, we can control the behavior of Z also for these kind of moves if we consider proper sequences of subgraphs, which are constructed as follows.

(S1)

From
G_{0}
perform
m1
for any vertex of
G
adjacent to one of the vertices of
l
. At the end we obtain the subgraph
G^{(1)}
.

(S2)

Add all the remaining edges incident to
l
with moves
m2
, ending with a subgraph
G^{(2)}
.

(S3)

Add the rest of the edges with vertices in
V(G^{(1)})
with moves
m2
, obtaining the subgraph
G^{(3)}
.

(S4)

Perform
m1
for any remaining vertex , obtaining
G^{(4)}
.

(S5)

Complete to
G
adding the rest of the edges with moves
m2
.

What we do is to examine all the 5 steps described above. For what we said about
m1
, the steps (S1) and (S4) don’t change the value of Z with respect to
l
, so
Z(G^{(1)},l)=Z(G_{0},l)=2
and
Z(G^{(4)},l)=Z(G^{(3)},l)
. Also for (S5) it’s quite simple, because every edge
f
added in this step doesn’t create any new 3 or 4-cycle that contains
l
, so
f(G_{i+1},l,f)=4
and the other contributions
f(G_{i+1},l,l^{\prime})
don’t change, hence
Z(G_{i+1},l)=Z(G_{i},l)+4
. So at the end of the process we have

Z(G,l)=Z(G^{(4)},l)+4(|E|-|E(G^{(4)})|).

Next we study (S2). As it can be seen, all the 3-cycles of
G
containing
l
are constructed in this process, and moreover each move
m2
constructs a unique 3-cycle of this type. It’s also important to notice that here we do not construct any other cycle of length greater than 3. The next lemma explains what happens to Z in this process.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{i+1}
be a move in (S2). Then it holds that:

(a)

Z(G_{i+1},l)=Z(G_{i},l)-2
if it is the first move of (S2),

(b)

Z(G_{i+1},l)=Z(G_{i},l)
otherwise.

In particular we have that

Z(G^{(2)},l)=\begin{cases}2&\text{ if }G^{(2)}=G^{(1)}\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

We observed that a step
G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{i+1}
in (S2) adds an edge
f
which completes a 3-cycle with edges
\{l,f,g\}
, so
f(G_{i+1},l,g)=f(G_{i+1},l,f)=2
. Since in
G_{i}
the edge
g
is not in any 3-cycle with
l
,
f(G_{i},l,g)=4
, but notice that
f(G_{i+1},l,l^{\prime})=f(G_{i},l,l^{\prime})
for any
l^{\prime}\neq g
edge of
G_{i}
, giving us (using
5
)

\sum_{l^{\prime}\in E(G_{i})}f(G_{i+1},l,l^{\prime})=\sum_{l^{\prime}\in E(G_{i})}f(G_{i},l,l^{\prime})\,\,-2=\big|f(G_{i},l)\big|-2.

Hence, using again
5
,

\big|F(G_{i+1},l)\big|=f(G_{i+1},l,f)+\sum_{l^{\prime}\in E(G_{i})}f(G_{i+1},l,l^{\prime})=\big|F(G_{i},l)\big|.

From this we deduce that the only case when Z changes is when
\mathbb{1}_{E_{3}(G_{i+1})}(l)=1
and
\mathbb{1}_{E_{3}(G_{i})}(l)=0
. This happens exactly when the move
G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{i+1}
create a first 3-cycle containing
l
, hence when it is the first move of (S2).
∎

We study for last (S3), the most delicate one. Here a move
G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{i+1}
adds an edge
f
not adjacent to
l
which can either create a 4-cycle containing
l
or not. In the first case various situations may happens, which lead us to different behaviors of Z. First of all, we notice that if the induced subgraph of
G_{i+1}
(hence of
G
) with vertex set
V(l)\cup V(f)
properly contains a 4-cycle, then we have
Z(G_{i+1},l)\geq Z(G_{i},l)
, so for convenience we perform these types of moves at the beginning of (S3) and we call this sub process (S3a). After this, we consider another subprocess (S3b), in which we perform all the moves
m2
which creates induced 4-cycles with respect to its vertices: we call these induced 4-cycles of
G
containing
l
the
pages of

l
, and the set of all the pages of
l
will be denoted by
\mathcal{B}(l)
. It’s important to observe that the creation of a new page
P
with edges
\{l,f,g,h\}
lends us to three different situations respect to the behavior of Z.

i)

f(G_{i},l,g)=f(G_{i},l,h)=2
. Then we have that
Z(G_{i+1},l)=Z(G_{i},l)+f(G_{i+1},l,f)=Z(G_{i},l)+2
.

Observe that the only possible way in which we end up to this case are when both
g
and
h
are in different 4-cycles of
G_{i}
. We illustrates two examples which will appear in the future.

ii)

f(G_{i},l,g)=2
and

(or vice versa). Then we have that
f(G_{i+1},l,h)=2
, so

Z(G_{i+1},l)=Z(G_{i},l)-2+f(G_{i+1},l,f)=Z(G_{i},l).

Notice that this is the case when
h
is not involved in any 3 or 4-cycle of
G_{i}
, but
g
is in a 4-cycle of
G_{i}
.

. Then we have that

, hence

This is the case when

.

Finally, after (S3b) we take the moves which do not create pages of
l
for last, finishing (S3) with the subprocess (S3c). It’s better to observe that in a move of (S3c) we are in the same situation as in (S5), so we have
Z(G_{i+1},l)=Z(G_{i},l)+4
.
Let’s give a look at the behavior of Z during the process (S3) step by step. By what we said above on the subprocesses (S3a), given
G^{(3a)}
the graph obtained at the end of (S3a), it holds that

(9)

Z(G^{(3a)},l)\geq 2-2\cdot\mathbb{1}_{E_{3}}(l),

We want to find a lower bound for
Z(G^{(3b)},l)
, where
G^{(3b)}
is the graph obtained at the end of (S3b). In truth, we derive a lower bound which holds for every subgraph involved in this subprocess. In order to do this we define an equivalence relation
\approx
on the set of all 4-cycles of
G
containing
l
.

Given
P,Q
such cycles, we say that
P\sim Q
if they share exactly two edges (see Figure
8
for an example). Taking the transitive closure of
\sim
we obtain the equivalence relation
\approx
. Precisely,
P\approx Q
if there exists a sequence of 4-cycles
P=P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{n}=Q
in
G
such that
P_{i}\sim P_{i+1}
for all
i\in[n-1]
.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
l
r
g
s
h
f

Figure 8.
An example of
P\sim Q
, where
P=\{l,g,h,f\}
and
Q=\{l,g,r,s\}

Moreover, we denote by
B(G,l)
the number of the equivalence classes on the set of all 4-cycles of
G
containing
l
which do not contain a 4-cycle created in (S3a).

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
G_{i}
be a subgraph that is in the subprocess (S3b). Then it holds that

(10)

Z(G_{i},l)\geq 2-2\cdot\mathbb{1}_{E_{3}}(l)-2B(G_{i},l).

Proof.

We prove it by induction on the number of moves after the subprocess (S3a). The base step is when we are right at the end of (S3a), hence
B(G_{i},l)=0
and so the inequality above is exactly inequality
9
.

Now let
G_{i+1}
be in (S3b) obtained by adding an edge
f
in
G_{i}
, which is also in (S3b). We observe that
B(G_{i+1},l)
can have three possible behaviors in function of
B(G_{i},l)
.

(1)

B(G_{i+1},l)=B(G_{i},l)+1
. This means that the page in
G_{i+1}
created by
f
forms a different class, hence the move
G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{i+1}
is of the type illustrated by Figure
7
, so we have that
Z(G_{i+1},l)=Z(G_{i},l)-2
.

(2)

B(G_{i+1},l)=B(G_{i},l)
. This happens exactly when
f
creates a page
P
such that
P\sim Q
, for a page
Q
in
G_{i}
, so the move
G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{i+1}
is of the type of Figure
6
, hence we have that
Z(G_{i+1},l)\geq Z(G_{i},l)
.

(3)

B(G_{i+1},l)=B(G_{i},l)-1
. This is the case when either the page
P
created by
f
make a collapse of two different equivalence classes of pages in
G_{i}
in one in
G_{i+1}
, or there exists a 4-cycle
Q^{\prime}
in
G^{(3a)}
such that
P\sim Q^{\prime}
. Both of these scenarios are particular instances that fall in the cases of Figure
4
and Figure
5
, so we have that
Z(G_{i+1},l)=Z(G_{i},l)+2
.

Applying the induction hypothesis on
G_{i}
it’s clear that the inequality
10
is satisfied.
∎

Now, putting together what we said about the subprocesses of (S3), we obtain a lower bound on
Z(G^{(3)},l)
.

Lemma 3.7
.

The following inequality holds

(11)

Z(G^{(3)},l)\geq 2-2\cdot\mathbb{1}_{E_{3}}-2B+4C,

where
B=B(G,l)
and
C
is the number of moves in (S3c).

Now it suffices to integrate the relations of (S4) and (S5) to derive the bound on
Z(G,l)
.

Proposition 3.8
.

For any
G=(V,E)
connected graph and for any
l\in E
it holds

(12)

Z(G,l)\geq 2-2\cdot\mathbb{1}_{E_{3}}-2B+4N,

where
B
is as in Lemma
3.7
and
N
is the number of moves in (S3c) and in (S5).

We are now ready to prove Proposition
3.4
.

Proof of Proposition
3.4
:

Among all the edges of
G
, let us choose
l=\{v,w\}\in E
which maximizes
B=B(G,l)
. If
B=0
the result is immediate by Proposition
3.8
.
Suppose now that
B>0
; this implies that there exists a page
P
that is constructed in (S3b) respect to
l
and it is not equivalent to any 4-cycle constructed in (S3a), and take the edge
l^{\prime}
of
P
not adjacent to
l
. Proposition
3.8
gives the inequality
12
for
l
and the following inequality for
l^{\prime}

(13)

Z(G,l^{\prime})\geq 2-2\cdot\mathbb{1}_{E_{3}}-2B^{\prime}+4N^{\prime}.

We notice that any page
Q
with edges
\{l,g,h,f\}
(say
f
is not adjacent to
l
) that is not in the same class of
P
respect to
\approx
in
\mathcal{B}(l)
is such that
f
is not in a 4-cycle of
G
containing
l^{\prime}
, or
f
is in a 4-cycle of
G
containing
l^{\prime}
but
g
and
h
are not. In fact, if
f
is in a 4-cycle of
G
that contains
l^{\prime}
, then
G
should contain one of the two possible subgraphs illustrated in Figure
9
.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
P
Q
l
h
g
f
l^{\prime}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
P
Q
l
h
g
f
l^{\prime}

Figure 9.
The two possible subgraphs contained in
G

The first case would imply
P\approx Q
so it cannot happen. In the other case, note that
g
and
h
cannot be in 4-cycles containing
l^{\prime}
, because otherwise we get an edge that witnesses
P\approx Q
or that
P
is not a page, in contrast with our assumptions.

As a consequence we have that
N^{\prime}\geq B-1
and, vice versa,
N\geq B^{\prime}-1
. Moreover, if
l\in E_{3}
, then any 3-cycle of
G
containing
l
has an edge
g
which is not in any 4-cycle containing
l^{\prime}
: in fact, if this doesn’t hold, then locally
G
contains the subgraph given in Figure
10
, and this would imply that
P
is equivalent to a 4-cycle created in (S3a), in contrast with our assumptions.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
P
l
l^{\prime}

Figure 10.
The local structure of
G
if
g
and
h
are in 3 or 4-cycles of
G
containing
l^{\prime}
.

This says that if
l\in E_{3}
, then
N^{\prime}\geq B
(and the analogue if
l^{\prime}\in E_{3}
). This leads us to bound
Z(G,l)+Z(G,l^{\prime})
in all the possible cases:

(1)

l,l^{\prime}\in E_{3}
:

Z(G,l)+Z(G,l^{\prime})\geq 4(B+B^{\prime})-2(B+B^{\prime})=2(B+B^{\prime})\geq 2.

(2)

l\in E_{3}
,
l^{\prime}\not\in E_{3}
(and vice versa):

Z(G,l)+Z(G,l^{\prime})\geq 4(B+B^{\prime}-1)-2(B+B^{\prime}-1)=2(B+B^{\prime}-1)\geq 0.

(3)

l,l^{\prime}\not\in E_{3}
: from
l^{\prime}\not\in E_{3}
we have that
B^{\prime}\geq 1
, so

Z(G,l)+Z(G,l^{\prime})\geq 4(B+B^{\prime}-2)-2(B+B^{\prime}-2)=2(B+B^{\prime}-2)\geq 0.

We have to show now that for any edge
f
different to
l
it holds that
Z(G,f)\geq 0
. We notice that the argument above holds for any edge
f
in any page contained in one of the
B
special classes, giving in particular
Z(G,f)\geq 0
. For the other edges
f
, we say that for any of the
B
special equivalence classes of pages of
l
there is an edge which is not in any 4-cycle containing
f
. In order to show this, let’s pick a page
P
with edges
\{l,g,h,s\}
(
s
not adjacent to
l
) in a class mentioned above and let’s treat the various cases which depends on the type of the edge
f
.

Let
f
be in a 3-cycle with
l
. If
g
or
h
are in a 3 or 4-cycle with
f
, then
G
contains one of the two subgraphs illustrated in Figure
11
(up to isomorphism).

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
P

Figure 11.
The two possible subgraphs contained in

The first says that
P
is not a page and the second gives
P\sim Q
, where
Q
is the 4-cycle containing
f
and

, so it’s constructed in (S3a), against the assumptions given on
P
.

With the same argument it can be proven that for any 4-cycle
Q
that contains
f
and
l
, if

and

are in 3 or 4-cycles that contain

then

, and this settle down all the other cases when

is in a 4-cycle of

.

If

is added in (S3c), then there exist two edges

and

incident to

which insist to the same vertex of

, say

. But now, if there is a 4 cycle of

containing

and

, then there exists an edge

which contains the vertex
w
, and so there would be a 4-cycle with edges
f,e,l
and one between
r
and

, in contrast with our assumption on
f
.

When

is not an edge of
G^{(3)}
we have to distinguish two different situations, whether
f
is incident to

or not. When
f
is incident to
s
(hence without loss of generality
f
is also incident to

) if there is a 4-cycle in
G
containing both

and

, then

contains one of the two subgraphs given by Figure

.

When

, if we suppose that

, then we have that there is a 4-cycle containing

and

(which contradicts that

) or

.

, again in contrast with the assumptions on

.

Hence in all the cases cited above we can construct a sequence of subgraphs starting from
\{f\}
ending in
G
such that there are at least
B
edges added in the processes (S3c) or (S5), giving us

Z(G,f)\geq-2B(G,f)+4N(f)\geq-2B+4B\geq 2.

∎

4.
Characterization of graphs with
z_{2}(G)=0

The proof of Proposition
3.4
also helps us to characterize all the graphs
G
such that
z_{2}(G)=0
. Let
G
be such a graph. Given
l\in E
which maximizes
B=B(G,l)
, by the lower bounds on
Z(G,l)+Z(G,l^{\prime})
we derive that
B\leq 1
, and if we consider also the lower bound given at the end of the proof, for any edge
f
of
G
with
B(G,f)=1
we have that
f\not\in E_{3}
. Now thanks to Proposition
3.8
we deduce that
Z(G,f)=0
for any
f\in E
, and in order to obtain this it is necessary that
G=G^{(3b)}(f)
for any
f\in E
, so for any pair of
f,f^{\prime}
of different edges in
G
we have that there exists a 3 or 4-cycle which contains
f
and
f^{\prime}
. The following two lemmas treats separately the cases
B=1
and
B=0
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
G
be a connected graph with
n
vertices such that
z_{2}(G)=0
and there exists
l\in E
with
B(G,l)=1
. Then
G\cong K_{2,n-2}
.

Proof.

By what we said above, since there are no 3-cycles that contain
l
, for any edge
f
of
G
there is a page of
l
containing
f
, and by
B(G,l)=1
we have that all the pages of
l
are equivalent. Notice that for any
G
and
l
such that
B(G,l)=1
there exists a sequence of subgraphs starting from
\{l\}
ending in
G
in which there is a
j>0
such that
Z(G_{i},l)=2
for any
i<j
,
Z(G_{j},l)=0
, and
Z(G_{i},l)\leq Z(G_{i+1},l)
for any
i\geq j
. We can construct such a sequence in this way: once done the process (S1), we start (S3) with a move
G_{j-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{j}
which constructs a page
P
, and after this we perform all the moves
m2
which create pages
Q
such that
Q\sim P
. Then inductively, we perform a move
G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{i+1}
if the page
Q^{\prime}
created in
G_{i+1}
is such that
Q^{\prime}\sim Q
for a certain
Q
page in
G_{i}
. In particular, by the hypothesis
z_{2}(G)=0
, we have also that
Z(G_{i},l)=0
for any
i\geq j
.

We show that for any pages
Q,Q^{\prime}
of
l
we have
Q\sim Q^{\prime}
. In fact, if there are
Q,Q^{\prime}
such that
Q\not\sim Q^{\prime}
, by
Q\approx Q^{\prime}
we have that there exist
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k}
(
k\geq 1)
pages such that
Q=Q_{0}\sim Q_{1}\sim\cdots\sim Q_{k}\sim Q_{k+1}=Q^{\prime}
. Without loss of generality we suppose that
Q\sim P\sim Q^{\prime}
for a certain page
P
, hence we consider a sequence of subgraphs as above in which the first three pages constructed are, in order,
P
,
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
. Thus
G
has a subgraph of the form given by Figure
14
.

\bullet
\bullet
v
w
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
l
h
h^{\prime}
g
g^{\prime}

Figure 14.
Q\sim P\sim Q^{\prime}
with
Q\not\sim Q^{\prime}
, where
\{l,g,g^{\prime}\}\subseteq P
,
\{l,g,h\}\subseteq Q
,
\{l,g^{\prime},h^{\prime}\}\subseteq Q^{\prime}
.

By the hypothesis
h
and
h^{\prime}
have to stay in a 4-cycle of
G
, hence
\{v,w\}\in E
. But given the move
G_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{i+1}
that adds
\{v,w\}
, it can be easily seen that
Z(G_{i+1},l)\geq Z(G_{i},l)+2
, hence
Z(G,l)>0
, in contrast with our hypothesis.

By the fact that
Q\sim Q^{\prime}
for any
Q,Q^{\prime}
pages of
l
it follows that there is an edge
s
incident to
l
such that any page
P
of
l
contains also
s
. Since any edge of
G
is in a page of
l
we have that, given
v,w
the two non-common vertices of
l
and
s
,
\{x,v\}
,
\{x,w\}\in E
for any
x\in V\smallsetminus\{v,w\}
, hence
K_{2,n-2}\subseteq G
. Moreover notice that any edge of the form
f=\{x,y\}
with
x,y\in V\smallsetminus\{v,w\}
can’t be in
G
because
l,s,f
can’t be in a 4-cycle, hence we conclude.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
G
be a connected graph with
n
vertices (
n\geq 3
) such that
z_{2}(G)=0
and
B(G,l)=0
for any
l\in E
. Then
G\cong K_{1,1,n-2}
or
G\cong K_{n}
.

Proof.

Since
B(G,l)=0
for any
l\in E
, then
E_{3}=E
, so
n\geq 3
.
If
G\not\cong K_{1,1,n-2}
, then for any
l\in E
there is
f\in E
which is in a 4-cycle containing
l
but not in any 3-cycle of
G
containing
l
, and for convenience
f
is not incident to
l
. We want to show that for any
l=\{v,w\}\in E
and for any
x\in V\smallsetminus\{v,w\}
,
G
has the 3-cycle
\{v,w,x\}
. In fact, if it doesn’t hold, then there exists an
l=\{v,w\}
and a vertex
u
such that
\{u,v\}\in E
but
\{u,w\}\not\in E
. Since
G=G^{(3b)}(l)
there exists a 4-cycle containing
l,g
with vertices
\{u,v,w,x\}
, and since
B(G,l)=0
we can suppose that such 4-cycle is not a page, so
f=\{v,x\}\in E(G)
. Using that
G\not\cong K_{1,1,n-2}
, we find an
h=\{y,z\}\in E
not adjacent to
f
, and again by
B(G,f)=0
we can suppose that
f
and
h
are contained in a 4-cycle that is not a page. Depending on the intersection of
\{u,w\}
and
\{y,z\}
we fall into two cases.

i)

\{u,w\}\cap\{y,z\}\neq\varnothing
. Then
G
contains a subgraph
H
with
5
vertices given in Figure
15
.

\bullet
w
\bullet
z
\bullet
u(=y)
\bullet
v
\bullet
x
f
l
h

Figure 15.
\{u,w\}\cap\{y,z\}\neq\varnothing
.

ii)

\{u,w\}\cap\{y,z\}=\varnothing
. Then
G
has a subgraph with
6
vertices given in Figure
16
.

\bullet
u
\bullet
w
\bullet
z
\bullet
y
\bullet
v
\bullet
x
f
l
h

Figure 16.
\{u,w\}\cap\{y,z\}=\varnothing
.

We observe that, given
G^{\prime}
an induced subgraph of
G
with 5 vertices which contains an isomorphic copy of
H
as subgraph, for any
e
edge of
G^{\prime}
we can construct a sequence of subgraphs starting from
\{e\}
ending in
G
such that
Z(G_{j},e)=0
for some
j>0
,
Z(G_{i},e)
is weakly increasing for any
i\geq j
and
Z(G_{k},e)=Z(G^{\prime},e)
for some
k>j
; the construction is given by performing (S1), (S2), and then we add all the remaining edges of
G^{\prime}
at the beginning of (S3).

We can verify with pure computations that a graph
G^{\prime}
with 5 vertices that contains (an isomorphic copy of)
H
is such that
Z(G^{\prime},e)=0
for any
e\in E(G^{\prime})
if and only if
G^{\prime}
is the complete graph
K_{5}
. So, if we come back to the case i), the induced subgraph of
G
with vertices
\{u,v,w,x,z\}
has to be
K_{5}
, in contrast with the fact that
\{u,w\}\not\in E
. We end up to the same contradiction in the case ii), applying this argument first with the induced subgraph with vertices
\{v,w,x,y,z\}
, then with the subgraph with vertex set
\{u,v,w,x,y\}
.

Once we have that for any
l=\{v,w\}\in E
and for any
x\in V\smallsetminus\{v,w\}
the 3-cycle
\{v,w,x\}
is in
G
, it is simple to show that
G\cong K_{n}
.
∎

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
G=([n],E)
be a connected graph. Then
z_{2}(G)=0
if and only if either
G\cong K_{n}
,
G\cong K_{1,1,n-2}
or
G\cong K_{2,n-2}
.

5.
Connection to the Ehrhart theory of symmetric edge polytopes

]
our goal is to investigate the effect of deleting an edge in the graph

on the

-polynomials

. We thus fix an edge

This is enough to prove
\gamma
-positivity of the
h^{*}
-polynomial of symmetric edge polytopes as it has been exploited in
[
DJKKV23
]
for the quadratic coefficient, as we prove in the following proposition.

Proposition 5.2
.

5.1
implies the Oshugi–Tsuchiya conjecture (
1.1
).

Proof.

Since every graph decomposes uniquely into its 2-connected components and
[
OT21a
, Proposition 5.2]
implies that the corresponding
h^{*}
-polynomial behaves multiplicative with respect to this decomposition, we can reduce
1.1
to the case of 2-connected graphs.

We apply induction on the cyclomatic number

\mathrm{cy}(G):=\left\lvert E\right\rvert-\left\lvert V\right\rvert+1

of a 2-connected graph
G=(V,E)
. For the base case, we assume that
\mathrm{cy}(G)=0
. Thus,
G
is a tree and the corresponding
h^{*}
-polynomial is known to be
\gamma
-nonnegative (see, for example,
[
OT21a
, Example 5.1]
).

Now, pick an edge
e\in E
that satisfies
5.1
. Since

\mathrm{cy}(G)=\mathrm{cy}(G\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to3.6pt{\vbox to6.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-0.3pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\hbox{\hbox to3.6pt{\vbox to6.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-0.3pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L 0 5.53}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

is

-nonnegative. The proof follows from

and the fact that

-positivity is preserved by taking linear combinations.

, we turn our attention to a special family of simplices contained in there. We are in particular interested in their

-polynomials, since they will play a fundamental role in our use of the Betke-McMullen formula (

) in the next subsection.

be an edge of
G
and

visible from the point

. By Corollary

, the associated oriented subgraph

is an oriented spanning tree inside

. We now define the (non necessarily unimodular) simplex
\hat{\sigma}:=\text{conv}\{\sigma\cup\{e_{ij}\}\}
, obtained by adding the vertex
e_{ij}
to
\sigma
. The oriented subgraph
G(\hat{\sigma})
contains a unique cycle, namely
\vec{P}_{\sigma}\cup\vec{ij}
, where
\vec{P}_{\sigma}
is contained in
G(\sigma)
.

We are interested in computing the
h^{*}
- and
\ell^{*}
-polynomials of
\hat{\sigma}
. To do so, we introduce the following parameters.

\displaystyle a_{\sigma}:

\displaystyle=\text{ number of edges of }\vec{P}_{\sigma}\text{ oriented against }\vec{ij}\text{ when traversing the cycle }\vec{P}_{\sigma}\cup\vec{ij},

\displaystyle b_{\sigma}:

\displaystyle=\text{ number of edges of }\vec{P}_{\sigma}\text{ oriented like }\vec{ij},

\displaystyle l_{\sigma}:

\displaystyle=a_{\sigma}+b_{\sigma}=\text{ length of }\vec{P}_{\sigma},

\displaystyle h_{\sigma}:

\displaystyle=a_{\sigma}-b_{\sigma}=\text{ number of lattice hyperplanes parallel to }\sigma\text{ between }e_{ij}\text{ and }\sigma.

\bullet
i
\bullet
j
\bullet
i_{1}
\bullet
i_{2}
\bullet
i_{3}

Figure 17.
Unique cycle in
T_{\sigma}\cup\vec{ij}
. Edges in red contribute to
a_{\sigma}
, while blue to
b_{\sigma}
.

Remark 5.3
.

Note that the condition that
e_{ij}
is visible from
\sigma
guarantees that
h_{\sigma}\geq 1
, precisely because it can be interpreted as the number of lattice hyperplanes between
e_{ij}
and
\sigma
. This follows from
Theorem

2.5
: if the equation for the facet on which
\hat{\sigma}
is lying is
\sum_{v\in V}f(v)x_{v}=1
, then the point
e_{ij}
lies on the translate of this hyperplane with right hand side equal to
f(j)-f(i)
. Since the orientation of an edge in
\sigma
corresponds to whether the function
f
is increasing or decreasing by
1
from the source vertex to the target vertex, we have that
f(j)-f(i)=a_{\sigma}-b_{\sigma}
.

With these in hand, we can give a formula for the
\ell^{*}
-polynomial of
\hat{\sigma}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\sigma
be a unimodular simplex contained in a facet of
P_{G\setminus e}
visible from the point
e_{ij}
. Let
\hat{\sigma}=\text{conv}\{\sigma\cup\{e_{ij}\}\}
. Then

\ell^{*}_{\hat{\sigma}}(t)=\begin{cases}t^{b_{\sigma}+2}+\dots+t^{a_{\sigma}-1}&\text{if }G(\hat{\sigma})\text{ is a cycle and }a_{\sigma}-b_{\sigma}\geq 3\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Let us denote by
\vec{E}(\sigma)
the collection of oriented edges of the tree
G(\sigma)
. Then we can express the fundamental parallelepiped as follows

(14)

\overset{\circ}{\Pi}(\hat{\sigma})=\left\{\left(\lambda_{ij}(e_{j}-e_{i})+\sum_{\vec{kl}\in\vec{E}(\sigma)}\lambda_{kl}(e_{l}-e_{k}),\,\lambda_{ij}+\sum_{\vec{kl}\in\vec{E}(\sigma)}\lambda_{kl}\right)\in\mathbbmss{R}^{d+1}\Bigg|\,\lambda_{ij},\lambda_{kl}\in(0,1)\right\}.

If
\vec{kl}
is an edge ending in a leaf of
G(\hat{\sigma})
, say
l
is the leaf, then the
l
-th coordinate of any point in
\overset{\circ}{\Pi}(\hat{\sigma})
is
\lambda_{kl}
, since
e_{l}
only appears in the summand
e_{l}-e_{k}
. Since
\lambda_{kl}\in(0,1)
, it follows that there are no integer points in
\overset{\circ}{\Pi}(\hat{\sigma})
and thus
\ell^{*}(\hat{\sigma})=0
.

Thus the only simplices
\hat{\sigma}
which have
\ell^{*}(\hat{\sigma})\neq 0
are those for which the corresponding subgraph
G(\hat{\sigma})
is a cycle. In this case, let
i_{0}=i,i_{1},\dots,i_{d}=j,i_{0}
be the underlying cycle. Without loss of generality, suppose that the sequence
i_{0},i_{1},\dots,i_{d}
is just
0,1,\dots,d
. Consider a point
p\in\overset{\circ}{\Pi}
. Then
e_{t}
appears twice in the sum from
14
defining
p
, once with the coefficient
\pm\lambda_{t-1,t}
and once with
\pm\lambda_{t,t+1}
. If the edges
\{t-1,t\}
and
\{t,t+1\}
appear with opposite orientations in
G(\hat{\sigma})
, then the
t
-th coordinate of
p
is
p_{t}=\lambda_{t-1,t}+\lambda_{t,t+1}
, while if the two edges appear with the same orientation, we have
p_{t}=\lambda_{t-1,t}-\lambda_{t,t+1}
. Thus we have
p\in\mathbbmss{Z}^{d+1}
if and only if

•

the coefficient of all edges oriented like
\vec{ij}
in the cycle
G(\hat{\sigma})
is
\lambda\in(0,1)
, and

•

the coefficient of edges oriented against
\vec{ij}
is
1-\lambda
, and

•

\lambda\in\{\frac{1}{a_{\sigma}-b_{\sigma}-1},\frac{2}{a_{\sigma}-b_{\sigma}-1},\dots,\frac{a_{\sigma}-b_{\sigma}-2}{a_{\sigma}-b_{\sigma}-1}\}
.

The last condition is obtained by imposing that the last coordinate of
p
is an integer, which holds if and only if
\lambda(a_{\sigma}-b_{\sigma}-1)\in\mathbbmss{Z}
. In order for
\lambda
to lie in the interval
(0,1)
, we further need that
a_{\sigma}-b_{\sigma}\geq 3
.
∎

P_{G\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to3.6pt{\vbox to6.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-0.3pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.15 0 L 0 8.3}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\hbox{\hbox to3.6pt{\vbox to6.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-0.3pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.15 0 L 0 8.3}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\hbox{\hbox to2.45pt{\vbox to4.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.22499pt\lower-0.22499pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

. We can then define
\Delta^{ij}:=\Gamma^{ij}*e_{ij}
, that is, the simplicial complex whose maximal
simplices are the convex hull of a maximal simplex of
\Gamma^{ij}
together with
e_{ij}
. This simplicial complex indeed triangulates the closure of the connected component of
P_{G}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to3.6pt{\vbox to6.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-0.3pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.15 0 L 0 8.3}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\hbox{\hbox to3.6pt{\vbox to6.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-0.3pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.15 0 L 0 8.3}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\hbox{\hbox to2.45pt{\vbox to4.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.22499pt\lower-0.22499pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\hbox{\hbox to1.9pt{\vbox to3.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.15 0 L 0 8.3}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\hbox{\hbox to3.6pt{\vbox to6.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-0.3pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.15 0 L 0 8.3}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\hbox{\hbox to2.45pt{\vbox to4.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.22499pt\lower-0.22499pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L 0 5.53}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\hbox{\hbox to1.9pt{\vbox to3.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.08 0 L 0 4.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}ij}
containing
e_{ij}
and can therefore be used to express the difference of
h^{*}
-polynomials.
Note that by construction all simplices in
\Delta^{ij}
are simplices of the form described in

Section

5.1
. This allows us to express the difference of the
h^{*}
-polynomials
after deleting an edge as follows.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
G
be a graph and
\Gamma^{ij}
and
\Delta^{ij}
be the triangulations described above. Then we have

h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)-h^{*}_{P_{G\setminus ij}}(t)=2th_{\Gamma^{ij}}(t)+2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\in\Delta^{ij}\\
G(F)\text{ cycle with }\\
a_{F}-b_{F}\geq 3\end{subarray}}h_{\mathrm{lk}_{\Delta^{ij}}(F)}(t)(t^{b_{F}+2}+\dots+t^{a_{F}-1}).

Proof.

We denote the support of the complex
\Delta^{ij}
by
|\Delta^{ij}|
.
The set
P_{G}\setminus P_{G\setminus ij}
is composed of two symmetric pieces, one of which can be written as
|\Delta^{ij}|\setminus|\Gamma^{ij}|
. Thanks to the additivity of the Ehrhart series, and because all these objects are
n
-dimensional except for
\Gamma^{ij}
, which is
n-1
dimensional, we have the following equality of their
h^{*}
-polynomials:

\frac{1}{2}(h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)-h^{*}_{P_{G\setminus ij}}(t))=h^{*}_{|\Delta^{ij}|}(t)-(1-t)h^{*}_{|\Gamma^{ij}|}(t)=h^{*}_{|\Delta^{ij}|}(t)-(1-t)h_{\Delta^{ij}}(t),

where the second equality follows since
\Gamma^{ij}
is a unimodular triangulation (so
h^{*}_{|\Gamma^{ij}|}(t)=h_{\Gamma^{ij}}(t)
), and because
\Delta^{ij}
is a cone over
\Gamma^{ij}
, we have that
h_{\Delta^{ij}}(t)=h_{\Gamma^{ij}}(t)
.

Applying Betke-McMullen (
Theorem

2.2
) and
Lemma

5.4
we have

\displaystyle h^{*}_{|\Delta^{ij}|}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{F\in\Delta^{ij}}h_{\mathrm{lk}_{\Delta^{ij}}(F)}(t)\ell^{*}_{F}(t)

\displaystyle=h_{\Delta^{ij}}(t)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\in\Delta^{ij}\\
G(F)\text{ cycle with }\\
a_{F}-b_{F}\geq 3\end{subarray}}h_{\mathrm{lk}_{\Delta^{ij}}(F)}(t)\ell^{*}_{F}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\in\Delta^{ij}\\
G(F)\text{ cycle with }\\
a_{F}-b_{F}\geq 3\end{subarray}}h_{\mathrm{lk}_{\Delta^{ij}}(F)}(t)(t^{b_{F}+2}+\dots+t^{a_{F}-1})

where the
h_{\Delta^{ij}}
in the second line comes from
F=\varnothing\in\Delta^{ij}
, since by convention
\ell^{*}_{\varnothing}(t)=1
.
By combining the two equations, we obtain the desired formula.
∎

5.3.
Connection to
z_{2}(G)

We now highlight a connection of
Lemma

5.5
with the lower bound of the number of edges of
P_{G}
discussed in
Section

3
.

Let
G=([n],E)
be a
2
-connected graph, and consider the polynomials
c^{ij}(t):=h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)-h^{*}_{P_{G\setminus ij}}(t)
, where
ij\in E
. Note that since
G
is
2
-connected,
c^{ij}(t)
is still a palindromic polynomial with the same center as the two polynomials
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)
and
h^{*}_{P_{G\setminus ij}}(t)
.

An approach to
1.1
is to to prove that there exists an edge
ij
in
G
for which the
\gamma
-polynomial associated with
c^{ij}(t)
has nonnegative coefficients. To this end, we propose to investigate the following stronger statement: consider the sum over the edges
ij
of
G
of the polynomials
c^{ij}(t)
. The result is a palindromic polynomial with the same center, whose associated
\gamma
-polynomial is the sum of the
\gamma
-polynomials of each
c^{ij}(t)
. In particular, the
\gamma
-nonnegativity of this sum would certify the existence of an edge
ij\in E
for which
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)-h^{*}_{P_{G\setminus ij}}(t)
is
\gamma
-nonnegative.

Conjecture 5.6
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
2
-connected graph. The polynomial

Z_{G}(t):=\sum_{ij\in E}\gamma(c^{ij}(t))=\sum_{ij\in E}\gamma(h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)-h^{*}_{P_{G\setminus ij}}(t))=\sum_{ij\in E}\gamma_{P_{G}}(t)-\gamma_{P_{G\setminus ij}}(t)

has nonnegative coefficients.

In particular, this conjecture implies
1.1
. We have checked the validity of
5.6
for all
2
-connected graphs with up to
8
vertices using a computer.

Example 5.7
.

Let
G=K_{n}
, for some
n\geq 3
. In this case
G\setminus ij
is, up to isomorphism, independent on the choice of
ij\in E
. We can determine the facets of
P_{G\setminus ij}
which are visible from
e_{ij}
. By
Theorem

2.5
facets of
P_{G\setminus ij}
correspond to certain maps
f:[n]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. The ones visible from
e_{ij}
are precisely the maps in
Theorem

2.5
for which
f(j)-f(i)>1
. For
G=K_{n}
, by condition
(i)
in
Theorem

2.5
this is equivalent to
f(j)-f(i)=2
, which in turn implies that
f(j)-f(k)=f(k)-f(i)=1
for every
k\in[n]\setminus{i,j}
. In particular there is, up to translation, only one map satisfying the conditions in
Theorem

2.5
, and hence there is a unique facet
F
of
P_{G\setminus ij}
visible from
e_{ij}
,
F=\text{conv}\{e_{i}-e_{k},e_{k}-e_{j}~:~k\in[n]\setminus\{i,j\}\}
.
In order to compute
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)-h^{*}_{P_{G\setminus ij}}(t)
,
we use a HJM triangulation of
F
described in
Proposition

2.7
. Fix an order on the edges of
G\setminus ij
and assume that
iv
is the minimum among all the edges of the form
ik
and
kj
with
k\in[n]\setminus\{i,j\}\}
. The case in which the minimal edge is of the form
vj
is completely analogous. The maximal simplices in the corresponding unimodular triangulation
\Gamma^{ij}
of
F
are the convex hull of subsets
S\subseteq\{e_{i}-e_{k},e_{k}-e_{j}~:~k\in[n]\setminus\{i,j\}\}
such that:

-

e_{i}-e_{v}\in S
and
e_{v}-e_{j}\in S
;

-

exactly one between
e_{i}-e_{k}
and
e_{k}-e_{j}
is in
S
, for every
k\in[n]\setminus\{i,j,v\}
.

In particular,
\Gamma^{ij}
is combinatorially equivalent to the join of a
(n-4)
-dimensional cross-polytope and a
1
-simplex. Hence
h_{\Gamma^{ij}}(t)=(1+t)^{n-3}
. By
Lemma

5.5
we have that

h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)-h^{*}_{P_{G\setminus ij}}(t)=2th_{\Gamma^{ij}}(t)=2t(1+t)^{n-3}.

Since
\gamma(2t(1+t)^{n-3})=2t
, we conclude that
Z_{K_{n}}(t)=\binom{n}{2}2t=n(n-1)t
.

Example 5.8
.

Let
G=C_{n}
, be the
n
-cycle for some
n\geq 3
. Then its
h^{*}
is computed in
[
OT21a
]
.
Deleting any edge yields a tree on
n
vertices, whose symmetric edge polytope is unimodularly equivalent to the standard cross polytope
\Diamond_{n-1}
, and therefore its
h^{*}
-polynomial is
h^{*}_{\Diamond_{n-1}}=(t+1)^{n-1}
.

We thus obtain

\displaystyle Z_{C_{n}}(t)=

\displaystyle\,n(h^{*}_{P_{C_{n}}}(t)-h^{*}_{\Diamond_{n-1}}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,n\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\rfloor}\binom{2i}{i}t^{i}(t+1)^{n-1-2i},

which is
\gamma
-nonnegative.

We now want to show how one can use
Lemma

5.5
to give explicit expressions for the first coefficients of
c^{ij}=\sum_{0}^{d}a_{i}t^{i}
, and then combine these to give expressions for the first coefficients of
Z_{G}
. We easily see that
a_{0}=0
and
a_{1}=2
. With a bit more work we can find
a_{2}
. To do so, we need intermediate results about the triangulation
\Gamma^{ij}
.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
G=([n],E)
be a 2-connected graph. Let
\gamma
be one of the shortest paths in
G
from
i
to
j
, and let
\Gamma
be the HJM triangulation induced by an order
<
for which the edges of
\gamma
are smaller than any edge not in
\gamma
Then, any induced oriented path
\vec{\gamma}
is in
\Gamma
.

Proof.

We have to prove that
\prod_{e\in\vec{\gamma}}p_{e}\not\in\text{in}_{<}(I_{P_{G}})
.
Let
k
be the length of
\gamma
. There cannot be a
2k
-cycle or a
(2k-1)
-cycle that contains
\gamma
which produces a binomial of type (1) or (2) in Proposition
2.7
with leading term
\prod_{e\in\vec{\gamma}}p_{e}
, because of the minimality of
\gamma
.

Suppose now that
\prod_{e\in\vec{\gamma}}p_{e}
is the multiple of a generator of
\text{in}_{<}(I_{P_{G}})
. By Proposition
2.7
, there exists a subset
S
of edges of
\vec{\gamma}
such that
\prod_{e\in S}p_{e}\in\text{in}_{<}(I_{P_{G}})
.

Let
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
be respectively the first and the last vertices in
\gamma
which are also in
C_{S}
, then consider the two paths
\tau_{1}
,
\tau_{2}
from
v_{1}
to
v_{2}
that form
C_{S}
. By the minimality of
\gamma
,
\ell(\tau_{i})\geq\ell(\gamma_{|v_{1},v_{2}})
for
i=1,2
, and since
S\subseteq\gamma_{|v_{1},v_{2}}
we have that
|C_{S}|=\ell(\tau_{1})+\ell(\tau_{2})\geq 2|S|
, hence we are in the case (1) of Proposition
2.7
, and this implies that

\ell(\tau_{1})=\ell(\tau_{2})=\ell(\gamma_{|v_{1},v_{2}})
and
S=\gamma_{|v_{1},v_{2}}
.

Let
l
be the minimal edge that appears in
C_{S}
and suppose without loss of generality that
l\in\tau_{1}
; by the hypothesis
l\not\in S
.
Now the path that goes from
i
to
v_{1}
by
\gamma
, from
v_{1}
to
v_{2}
by
\tau_{1}
and from
v_{2}
to
j
by
\gamma
would be less than
\gamma
, in contrast with the assumptions on the minimality of
\gamma
.
∎

We now focus on paths oriented entirely from
i
to
j
.

. Then there is a unique simplex

, that is,

.

and oriented correctly. We now prove uniqueness of such a face. Suppose to the contrary that there were two different paths oriented from
i
to
j
which correspond to faces of
\Gamma^{ij}
. Let
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
respectively be the first vertex where the paths diverge and where they first meet again, and call
\vec{P_{1}}
and
\vec{P_{2}}
the restriction of those oriented paths between

and
i_{2}
. Suppose that
\vec{P_{1}}
is at most as long as
\vec{P_{2}}
, and consider the oriented cycle
C
obtained as the union of
\vec{P_{2}}
and the edges of
\vec{P_{1}}
oriented inversely. By
Proposition

2.7
, if we choose as
I
any subset of the oriented edges of
\vec{P_{2}}
of size
\lfloor|P_{1}\cup P_{2}|/2\rfloor
, the set
I
forms a non-face of
\Gamma^{ij}
, and therefore so does the original oriented path it is a subset of, which yields a contradiction.
∎

We can now give an explicit formula for the quadratic coefficient of
c^{ij}(t)

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
G
be a
2
-connected graph with
n
vertices and
ij
an edge in
G
. Then the coefficient of
t^{2}
in
c^{ij}(t)
is

(15)

a_{2}=\begin{cases}2(f_{0}(\Gamma^{ij})-n+1),&\text{ if shortest path in }G\setminus ij\text{ has length }2,\\
2(f_{0}(\Gamma^{ij})-n+2),&\text{ if shortest path in }G\setminus ij\text{ has length }\geq 3.\end{cases}\\

Proof.

We use the formula from
Lemma

5.4
and analyze the quadratic term to obtain the following expression:

a_{2}=2h_{1}(\Gamma^{ij})+2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\in\Delta^{ij}\\
G(F)\text{ cycle with }\\
b_{F}=0,\,a_{F}\geq 3\end{subarray}}1

Thanks to
Lemma

5.10
, we know that there is a unique
F\in\Delta^{ij}
with
b_{F}=0
. We then see that the sum in the expression above is empty if the unique facet
F
of
\Gamma^{ij}
with
b_{F}=0
satisfies
a_{F}<3
and
1
otherwise. We conclude by observing that
h_{1}(\Gamma^{ij})=f_{0}(\Gamma^{ij})-n+1
.
∎

Because
c^{ij}(t)
is a palindromic polynomial, we can rewrite it in the
\gamma
-basis; let
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots
be the coefficients in this basis. Then knowing the three coefficients
a_{0},a_{1}
and
a_{2}
of the polynomial in the standard monomial basis allows us to derive a formula for
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}
and more interestingly for
\gamma_{2}
. .

(16)

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}=

\displaystyle a_{2}-(a_{1}-(n-1)a_{0})(n-3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}2(f_{0}(\Gamma^{ij})-2n+4),&\text{ if shortest path in }G\setminus ij\text{ has length }2,\\
2(f_{0}(\Gamma^{ij})-2n+5),&\text{ if shortest path in }G\setminus ij\text{ has length }\geq 3.\end{cases}

The next lemma gives the key to interpreting these numbers.

, if

were not an edge of

, then we must have that

is in the convex hull of

and certain other vertices of
P_{G}
, but this is not possible, since it is a vertex of
P_{G}
.
∎

Let
Z_{G}(t)=\sum_{i}z_{i}t^{i}
. It is easy to verify that
z_{0}=0
and
z_{1}=2|E|
. We now show that the quadratic coefficient
z_{2}
of
Z_{G}(t)
is tightly related with the bound given in
Section

3
. Indeed we have that

(17)

\displaystyle z_{2}=\sum_{ij\in E}\gamma_{2}(c^{ij}(t))=

\displaystyle\sum_{ij\in E}|N_{P_{G}}(e_{ij})|+2|E|(2n-5)-2|E_{3}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2f_{1}(P_{G})+2|E|(2n-5)-2|E_{3}|=z_{2}(G),

where
E_{3}\subseteq E
is the set of all edges of
G
which are contained in a cycle, and
z_{2}(G)
the quantity defined in (
3
). The second equality is thanks to a simple double counting, since each vertex of each edge of
P_{G}
is counted once, as the neighbor of the other vertex.

Theorem

3.1
then directly implies the following result, which was originally proved in
[
DJKKV23
, Theorem 3.2]
.

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
2
-connected graph. Then there exists an edge
ij\in E
such that
\gamma_{2}(h^{*}_{P_{G}})-\gamma_{2}(h^{*}_{P_{G\setminus ij}})\geq 0
.

6.
Further directions

The formula in
Lemma

5.5
allows us to write
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)-h^{*}_{P_{G\setminus ij}}(t)
as the sum of polynomials, all of which have nonnegative coefficients. Unfortunately, these summands are not palindromic, so this decomposition does not yield an analog formula for the
\gamma
-polynomial of
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)-h^{*}_{P_{G\setminus ij}}(t)
. During our investigation we observed a second intriguing formula for this difference, which we offer as a conjecture.

Let
\Gamma
be a unimodular triangulation of the subcomplex of
P_{G\setminus ij}
consisting of all facets visible by
e_{ij}
, and consider

L:=\{h_{F}\,|\,F\text{ facet of }\Gamma\}=\{h_{1}>h_{2}>\cdots>h_{k}\}

be the set of all possible lattice distances from
e_{ij}
to a facet of
\Gamma
in decreasing order.

For
s=1,\ldots,k
we define

\Gamma_{s}:=\langle\sigma\text{ facet of }\Gamma\,|\,h_{\sigma}\geq h_{s}\rangle,

the subcomplex of
\Gamma
generated by the facets with lattice distance at least
h_{s}
from
e_{ij}
. Observe that by definition
\Gamma_{s}
is a subcomplex of
\Gamma_{s+1}
, for every
s=1,\dots,k-1
.

The following conjecture predicts that we can write
h^{*}_{P_{G}}(t)-h^{*}_{P_{G\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox to3.6pt{\vbox to6.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-0.3pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.15 0 L 0 8.3}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\hbox{\hbox to3.6pt{\vbox to6.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-0.3pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

is not
\gamma
-nonnegative. Indeed, the
3
-dimensional complex
\Gamma^{13}
has 3 facets at lattice distance

from
e_{13}
and we have that

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}13}}(t)=2t(1+t+t^{2})=2t(1+t)^{2}-2t^{2},

whose associated
\gamma
-polynomial has a negative coefficients.
If we consider the edge
12
then the corresponding complex
\Gamma^{12}
has
3
facets all at lattice distance
1
from
e_{12}
. We can verify that

c^{12}(t)=2t(1+4t+t^{2})=2t(1+t)^{2}+4t^{2}.

The same holds for the edge
23
. Finally, if we consider the edge
34
we have that
\Gamma^{34}
has
4
facets, two at lattice distance
1
from
e_{34}
and two at lattice distance
2
from
e_{34}
. Thus we have an example where the sum in
Equation

18
has more than one term. As predicted by
6.1
we have that

c^{34}(t)=2t((1+t)(1+t)+(2t))=2t(1+t)^{2}+4t^{2},

and note that not just the whole sum, but all the summands of
Equation

18
are palindromic. The same holds for the edges
45
and
15
.

We illustrate
6.1
in a larger example.

is
\gamma
-nonnegative, the first summand is not
\gamma
-nonnegative.

The following question is also motivated by computations for graphs with few vertices, in which case the answer is positive. Recall that a pure simplicial complex is
shellable
if there is a total ordering
F_{1}<\dots<F_{m}
of its facets such that the intersection of each
F_{i}
with the complex generated by the facets
F_{1},\dots,F_{i-1}
is pure of codimension
1
. Remarkably, the coefficients of the
h
-vector of a shellable simplicial complex have a combinatorial interpretation (see for instance
[
Sta96
, III.2]
). This fact could be used to give a combinatorial interpretation for the polynomials
h_{\Gamma_{s}}(t)-h_{\Gamma_{s-1}}(t)
.

Question 6.4
.

Are the simplicial complexes
\Gamma_{s}
shellable for any
s=1,\ldots,k
? Is there a shelling order of
\Gamma
for which every facet of
\Gamma_{s}
is smaller than any facet of
\Gamma_{s+1}
, for every
s=1,\dots,k-1
?
